## User

I invoke the following skill:

<skill name="grilling">
---
name: grilling
description: Grill the user relentlessly about a plan, decision, or idea. Use when the user wants to stress-test their thinking, or uses any 'grill' trigger phrases.
---

Interview the user relentlessly until you reach a shared understanding. Map this as a **design tree**: every decision branches into the decisions that hang off it.

Work the tree in **rounds**. The **frontier** is every decision whose prerequisites are already settled: the questions you can ask _now_ without guessing at answers you haven't heard yet. Ask the whole frontier in one round: number each question and give your recommended answer. Then wait for the user's answers before the next round.

Format a round like so:

```
❓ **Q1** - **<question title>**: <question body, might be multiple paragraphs, including multiple choices>

➡️ <your recommended answer>

---

❓ **Q2** - **<question title>**: <question body, might be multiple paragraphs, including multiple choices>

➡️ <your recommended answer>
```

Word each question so "yes" accepts your recommended answer.

Each round the user answers reshapes the tree: settled decisions push the frontier outward and unblock questions that depended on them. Recompute the frontier and ask the next round. A question whose answer depends on another question still open in this round belongs to a _later_ round, not this one.

Finding _facts_ is your job, never the user's. When a frontier question needs a fact from the environment (filesystem, tools, etc.), dispatch a sub-agent to find it; don't ask the user for anything you could look up yourself. Don't block on it: a running exploration is an unsettled prerequisite, so only the questions downstream of it wait for the sub-agent to report; ask the rest of the frontier now. The _decisions_ are the user's: put each to them and wait.

The session is done when the frontier is empty: every branch of the design tree visited, nothing left silently assumed. Do not act on it until the user confirms you have reached a shared understanding.

</skill>

````

- path: prompt.md
  content: |
      - write a task queue and runner system to execute CLI command
      - the stdout, stderr of the commands are logged and can be viewed from a dashboard
      - the status of the task queues and runner system can be viewed from a dashboard
      - user add task with context to the system, tasks are extensible
      - user can specify a task folder to add to the look up
      - the task is looked up from the default and user task folders by id, like $PATH
      - each task should have README and settings
      - each registered task correspond to a task queue and can create task instance
      - the default function of the task will be executed as an task instance
      - each task's concurrency (number of task instance in RUNNING state) can be configured
      - write using Bun, use as much Bun built-in as possible
      - A Task has the following states:
        - PENDING
        - RUNNING
        - SUCCESS
        - FAILED
      - user can issue start/stop/abort processing per task queue
        start: start to process PENDING tasks
        stop: do complete any current RUNNING tasks, do not process next PENDING task
        abort: kill the current RUNNING task and set it to PENDING, do not process next PENDING task
      - The task queue hence has the following states:
        - IDLE
        - PROCESSING
        - STOPPED
      - sample commands (ls and minfo are task queue id)
      
        ```sh
        # start the service
        # registered tasks should autostart
        tq daemon
      
        tq add ls fixture/1
        tq add ls fixture/2
        tq add minfo fixture/1
        tq add minfo fixture/2
      
        tq stop ls
        tq start ls
        tq abort minfo
      
        tq clear ls # clear SUCCESS tasks
        tq clear --all ls # clear all tasks
        ```
      
      the design is not final, please assist me with it
      You may suggest better way to improve my design
      I don't have the following thought through
      
      - how to specify the input format (the context) of a task? should the task defines a convention?
      - how should a plugin expose multiple task?
      - how should multiple tasks share common module
      
      I would like you to write a specification with this prompt as input
      and log issues we would further discuss and clarify
````

```
- path: tasks/minfo.ts
  content: |
      import { $ } from "bun";
      
      function padZero(num: number, size: number): string {
        let s = num.toString();
        while (s.length < size) {
          s = "0" + s;
        }
        return s;
      }
      
      export function prettyTime(seconds: number): string {
        const hours = Math.floor(seconds / 3600);
        const minutes = Math.floor((seconds % 3600) / 60);
        const secs = Math.floor(seconds % 60);
      
        return `${padZero(hours, 2)}:${padZero(minutes, 2)}:${padZero(secs, 2)}`;
      }
      
      export function prettyBps(bps: string | number): string {
        let bytesN = 0;
        if (typeof bps == "string") {
          bytesN = parseInt(bps, 10);
        } else {
          bytesN = bps;
        }
        if (bytesN === 0) return "0 B";
        const k = 1024;
        const sizes = ["B", "KB", "MB", "GB", "TB", "PB", "EB", "ZB", "YB"];
        const i = Math.floor(Math.log(bytesN) / Math.log(k));
        return `${parseFloat((bytesN / Math.pow(k, i)).toFixed(2))}${sizes[i]}ps`;
      }
      
      export function printMediaInfo(
        minfo: Record<string, any>,
        args: Record<string, any>,
      ): void {
        if (!minfo["media"]) return;
        const file = minfo["media"]["@ref"];
        const vtrack = minfo["media"]["track"].find(
          (track: any) => track["@type"] === "Video",
        );
        const atrack = minfo["media"]["track"].find(
          (track: any) => track["@type"] === "Audio",
        );
        if (!vtrack || !atrack) {
          if (args.debug) {
            console.log(`Skipping file: ${file}`);
          }
          return;
        }
      
        console.log(
          `${file}: ${prettyTime(atrack["Duration"])} | ${vtrack["Format"]} ${vtrack["Format_Profile"]}/${vtrack["Width"]}x${vtrack["Height"]}@${vtrack["FrameRate"]}/${prettyBps(vtrack["BitRate"])} | ${atrack["Format"]}/${prettyBps(atrack["BitRate"])}`,
        );
        // console.log(JSON.stringify(vinfos));
      }
      
      export const getMediainfo = async (filepath: string) => {
        const { stdout, stderr, exitCode } =
          await $`mediainfo --Output=JSON "${filepath}"`.nothrow().quiet();
        if (exitCode !== 0) {
          console.error(`Error processing file: ${filepath}`);
          console.error(stderr);
          return null;
        }
      
        const minfo = JSON.parse(stdout.toString()) as Record<string, any>;
        const vtrack = minfo["media"]["track"].find(
          (track: any) => track["@type"] === "Video",
        );
        if (vtrack) {
          if (!vtrack["BitRate"]) {
            vtrack["BitRate"] = vtrack["BitRate_Maximum"];
          }
        }
        const atrack = minfo["media"]["track"].find(
          (track: any) => track["@type"] === "Audio",
        );
        if (atrack) {
          if (!atrack["BitRate"]) {
            atrack["BitRate"] = vtrack["BitRate_Nominal"];
          }
        }
      
        return minfo;
      };
      
      export default async (filepath: string) => {
        const minfo = await getMediainfo(filepath);
        if (!minfo) return -1;
        printMediaInfo(minfo, { debug: true });
        return 0;
      };
```

Now I have a clear picture of the project. Let me note what I found before starting the grilling rounds:

**Facts on the ground:**
- Bun project, ESM, TS 7, strict mode with verbatimModuleSyntax
- Two sample task plugins already exist in `tasks/`: `ls.ts` and `minfo.ts` — both export a `default` async function taking a single `filepath: string` argument
- `minfo.ts` also exports helper functions (`getMediainfo`, `printMediaInfo`, etc.)
- Fixture files: `fixture/1/1.srt`, `fixture/2/2.srt` (empty .srt files)
- CLI surface sketched in the prompt: `tq daemon`, `tq add <task-id> <context>`, `tq stop/start/abort <queue-id>`, `tq clear [--all]`
- Dashboard: stdout/stderr logging + queue/state viewer (no implementation yet)

---

I'll now grill you on the design tree. I'll present each round's **frontier** — the decisions I can ask now without guessing at answers I haven't heard. I start with recommended answers.

---

❓ **Q1** - **Process model: single daemon process vs. per-queue workers**: Should `tq daemon` run as a single long-lived process that owns all queues and polls/executes tasks itself, or should each task queue get its own worker process (spawned or separate)?

➡️ **Single daemon process** that owns all queues in one process, using Bun's built-in scheduling (e.g., `Bun.sleep`/`setInterval` or a lightweight event loop). One process to rule them all; concurrency per queue is a config knob inside that process.

---

❓ **Q2** - **Task identity and the "PATH-like" lookup**: You said tasks are looked up by ID from default + user task folders, "like `$PATH`". Two sub-questions folded in:

- **Q2a** — Is a *task ID* a single token (e.g., `ls`, `minfo`), or can it include a provider prefix (e.g., `builtin:ls`, `acme:encode`)? Said another way: do we need namespacing to let two plugins each expose a task called `convert`?
- **Q2b** — Is the *task folder* the unit of registration (one folder = one task, containing `default.ts` + `README.md` + `settings.json`), or can one folder register *multiple* tasks (a plugin)?

➡️ **Q2a**: Task IDs are single tokens for now (`ls`, `minfo`). If namespacing becomes necessary later we can add a `:` separator; don't pre-build it. ➡️ **Q2b**: One folder = one task, with `default.ts` as the entry point. A "plugin that exposes multiple tasks" is then modeled as multiple folders under a shared package, and shared modules are handled separately (see Q4).

---

❓ **Q3** - **Input/context convention**: How does a task declare what context it expects, and how is context passed at `tq add` time?

You explicitly flagged this as unthought-through: *"how to specify the input format (the context) of a task? should the task defines a convention?"*

- **Q3a** — Does the task declare its input schema (so the CLI/dashboard can validate), or is context just an opaque string/JSON blob the task must parse itself?
- **Q3b** — At `tq add ls fixture/1`, is `fixture/1` the *entire* context (a string), or is it parsed into a structured input (e.g., `{ filepath: "fixture/1" }`)? Who does the parsing — the CLI, the task registry, or the task itself?

➡️ **Q3a**: Tasks declare their input shape via a small convention — a `settings.json` (or a `schema.ts` export) that describes the input type. The task itself remains in charge of interpreting it, but the system knows the *shape* for validation, dashboard hints, and structured storage. ➡️ **Q3b**: `tq add` receives the context as a *JSON string* (or a file reference), and the registry parses/validates it against the task's declared schema before enqueueing. The task's `default` function receives the parsed, validated input object — so `ls`'s `default(filepath: string)` stays as-is because its declared input is `{ filepath: string }` and `tq add ls fixture/1` gets serialized/validated into that.

---

❓ **Q4** - **Sharing modules across tasks**: You flagged: *"how should multiple tasks share common module"*.

- **Q4a** — Is shared code a *package* (npm/bun dependency, symlinked into each task folder), a *monorepo-style* `tasks/lib/*.ts` imported via relative paths, or a *runtime module registry* the daemon loads once and injects?
- **Q4b** — If `minfo.ts` and a hypothetical `ffprobe.ts` both want `getMediainfo`-style helpers, where does that shared code live, and how do tasks import it without each task folder duplicating it?

➡️ **Q4a**: Treat shared code as normal TS modules under a `tasks/lib/` (or `tasks/shared/`) directory imported via relative paths from each task. One Bun process means no need for a runtime module registry; ES module resolution handles it. If a task genuinely needs to be its own redistributable package later, it can declare a bun dependency — but start with relative imports. ➡️ **Q4b**: Extract shared helpers into `tasks/lib/media.ts` (say), and have `minfo.ts` import from `../lib/media`. A task folder's import paths are resolved relative to the daemon's working directory / task search paths, so the daemon must add those roots to the module resolution path (Bun handles this naturally if tasks are loaded via `import()` from the right location).

---

❓ **Q5** - **Task "instance" vs. "task definition" and per-queue concurrency**: You said *"each registered task correspond to a task queue and can create task instance"* and *"each task's concurrency can be configured"*.

- **Q5a** — Is there **one queue per task definition** (so `ls` has queue `ls`, `minfo` has queue `minfo`), or can a user create **named queues** that route to a task definition (e.g., queue `nightly` runs task `minfo`)? The sample commands (`tq stop ls`, `tq abort minfo`) imply queue ID == task ID.
- **Q5b** — Concurrency is "number of task instances in RUNNING state". Is that concurrency **per queue** (so queue `ls` can run 3 `ls` instances concurrently, queue `minfo` can run 1), or **global** across all queues?

➡️ **Q5a**: Queue ID == task ID for now (one queue per registered task). Named routing queues are a later feature. ➡️ **Q5b**: Concurrency is **per queue**, configured in that task's `settings.json` (e.g., `"concurrency": 3`). The daemon respects each queue's limit independently.

---

❓ **Q6** - **Daemon persistence and "autostart"**: You said *"registered tasks should autostart"* and `tq daemon` starts the service.

- **Q6a** — Does "autostart" mean: on `tq daemon`, automatically begin processing PENDING tasks for all queues (i.e., each queue enters PROCESSING), or does it mean the daemon itself should restart on crash/reboot (a systemd-style autostart)?
- **Q6b** — Does the daemon persist queue/task state to disk (so a restart picks up enqueued tasks and their stdout/stderr logs), or is state in-memory only (tasks added via `tq add` are lost on daemon restart)?

➡️ **Q6a**: "Autostart" = on `tq daemon`, all registered queues automatically enter PROCESSING and start draining PENDING tasks. No systemd/reboot autostart for now. ➡️ **Q6b**: Persist to disk. Task instances, their state, and their stdout/stderr logs survive a daemon restart. The daemon re-reads the task store on startup; PENDING tasks resume. Logs go to a `logs/` directory (already in `.gitignore`).

---

❓ **Q7** - **Dashboard: CLI vs. HTTP**: You want a dashboard showing queue/state and logs.

- **Q7a** — Is the dashboard a **web UI** (HTTP server served by the daemon, e.g., `http://localhost:3000`), a **CLI reporter** (`tq status`, `tq logs <id>`), or both?
- **Q7b** — If web: does the daemon serve it, or is it a separate process? And do you want real-time updates (WebSocket/SSE) or periodic refresh?

➡️ **Q7a**: Both, but the web UI is the primary dashboard; CLI commands (`tq status`, `tq logs`) are the quick operational interface. ➡️ **Q7b**: Daemon serves a small HTTP server (Bun.serve) with a basic HTML dashboard; SSE for real-time state/log tailing. No separate process. Keep it minimal to start — a single `/` that lists queues/tasks and a `/logs/:id` stream.

---

❓ **Q8** - **Task lifecycle and the RUNNING→SUCCESS/FAILED transition**: You defined task states PENDING/RUNNING/SUCCESS/FAILED and queue states IDLE/PROCESSING/STOPPED.

- **Q8a** — When a task instance finishes, who sets the final state — the task function (returns an exit code / throws), or the runner wrapping the task? I.e., does `default(filepath)` returning `0` / throwing map to SUCCESS/FAILED?
- **Q8b** — For `abort`: you said "kill the current RUNNING task and set it to PENDING". Does "kill" mean terminate the child process (if the task spawns one) or cancel the Bun coroutine? And does the aborted task's partial output get preserved or discarded?

➡️ **Q8a**: The runner wraps the task. The task function returns a `number` exit code or throws; the runner maps exit 0 → SUCCESS, non-zero/throw → FAILED, and captures stdout/stderr regardless. The task itself doesn't set its own state. ➡️ **Q8b**: "Kill" = the daemon aborts the running Bun task (cancels its coroutine via `AbortSignal`/task interruption). If the task spawned a long-lived child process via `$`, the runner also kills that child. Partial stdout/stderr captured up to the abort point is preserved in the log (marked ABORTED or rolled back to PENDING per your spec — you said "set it to PENDING", so we keep the partial log but reset state to PENDING).

---

❓ **Q9** - **The `tq add` context parsing and the sample `ls`/`minfo` discrepancy**: The sample commands are:

```
tq add ls fixture/1
tq add minfo fixture/1
```

Both take a single positional argument that looks like a path. But `minfo.ts` does a lot more than `ls.ts` (calls `mediainfo`, parses JSON, formats output). This raises the question:

- **Q9a** — Should `tq add`'s second argument be a *path to a context file/dir* (the daemon reads it and passes the contents), or a *raw context string* (passed as-is to the task)?
- **Q9b** — Is the context for `ls` and `minfo` the same shape (`{ filepath: string }`), or does each task declare its own shape and the CLI doesn't care?

➡️ **Q9a**: The second argument is a *context value* — for simple tasks it's a string that the task's schema interprets; for structured context the user can pass JSON. The daemon does not auto-read files unless the task's schema says "this input is a file path" (in which case the task itself reads it). ➡️ **Q9b**: Each task declares its own input shape. `ls` and `minfo` happen to both want `{ filepath: string }`, but that's coincidence, not a system rule. The CLI passes the raw context string; the registry validates it against the task's schema at enqueue time.

---

That's round 1 — 9 questions, all on the frontier. I found the facts (existing task shape, Bun setup, sample commands) myself; everything above is a *decision* for you.

Answer in whatever form is easiest — yes/no on my recommendations, "yes on Q1/Q3 but no on Q5", or rewrite any recommendation outright. I'll recompute the frontier for round 2 from your answers.

````
- path: .agents/skills/setup-pre-commit/SKILL.md
  content: |
      ---
      name: setup-pre-commit
      description: Set up Husky pre-commit hooks with lint-staged (Prettier), type checking, and tests in the current repo. Use when user wants to add pre-commit hooks, set up Husky, configure lint-staged, or add commit-time formatting/typechecking/testing.
      ---
      
      # Setup Pre-Commit Hooks
      
      ## What This Sets Up
      
      - **Husky** pre-commit hook
      - **lint-staged** running Prettier on all staged files
      - **Prettier** config (if missing)
      - **typecheck** and **test** scripts in the pre-commit hook
      
      ## Steps
      
      ### 1. Detect package manager
      
      Check for `package-lock.json` (npm), `pnpm-lock.yaml` (pnpm), `yarn.lock` (yarn), `bun.lockb` (bun). Use whichever is present. Default to npm if unclear.
      
      ### 2. Install dependencies
      
      Install as devDependencies:
      
      ```
      husky lint-staged prettier
      ```
      
      ### 3. Initialize Husky
      
      ```bash
      npx husky init
      ```
      
      This creates `.husky/` dir and adds `prepare: "husky"` to package.json.
      
      ### 4. Create `.husky/pre-commit`
      
      Write this file (no shebang needed for Husky v9+):
      
      ```
      npx lint-staged
      npm run typecheck
      npm run test
      ```
      
      **Adapt**: Replace `npm` with detected package manager. If repo has no `typecheck` or `test` script in package.json, omit those lines and tell the user.
      
      ### 5. Create `.lintstagedrc`
      
      ```json
      {
        "*": "prettier --ignore-unknown --write"
      }
      ```
      
      ### 6. Create `.prettierrc` (if missing)
      
      Only create if no Prettier config exists. Use these defaults:
      
      ```json
      {
        "useTabs": false,
        "tabWidth": 2,
        "printWidth": 80,
        "singleQuote": false,
        "trailingComma": "es5",
        "semi": true,
        "arrowParens": "always"
      }
      ```
      
      ### 7. Verify
      
      - [ ] `.husky/pre-commit` exists and is executable
      - [ ] `.lintstagedrc` exists
      - [ ] `prepare` script in package.json is `"husky"`
      - [ ] `prettier` config exists
      - [ ] Run `npx lint-staged` to verify it works
      
      ### 8. Commit
      
      Stage all changed/created files and commit with message: `Add pre-commit hooks (husky + lint-staged + prettier)`
      
      This will run through the new pre-commit hooks: a good smoke test that everything works.
      
      ## Notes
      
      - Husky v9+ doesn't need shebangs in hook files
      - `prettier --ignore-unknown` skips files Prettier can't parse (images, etc.)
      - The pre-commit runs lint-staged first (fast, staged-only), then full typecheck and tests
      
  referencedBy: {}
- path: .agents/skills/wizard/SKILL.md
  content: |
      ---
      name: wizard
      description: Generate an interactive bash wizard that walks a human through steps only they can perform. Use when provisioning infrastructure, setting up credentials or CI secrets, walking an unfamiliar third-party dashboard, or running a one-off migration or cutover. Don't invoke this for steps the agent can perform itself.
      ---
      
      # Wizard
      
      A **wizard** is a bash script that walks a human, step by step, through a manual procedure that's tedious to do by hand and tedious to re-explain to an AI every time. It opens each URL, says exactly what to click and copy, captures the values, writes them where they belong (`.env`, GitHub secrets), confirms at every stage, and shows how many stages are left. It might configure third-party services, run a one-off migration, or move the project from one state to another.
      
      The delightful UX is already solved by [template.sh](template.sh): stage-by-stage progress, confirmation gates, cross-platform URL opening (including WSL), hidden secret entry, idempotent `.env` upserts, `gh secret`/`gh variable` writes, and a closing summary. **Your job is only to scope the procedure and author its stages.** The library above the `STAGES` marker is identical in every wizard; that consistency is the point: never hand-edit it.
      
      A wizard is ephemeral by default: built for one run, saved to a scratch or `scripts/` path, deleted when the job's done. Commit it only when the user wants a repeatable setup path that should live in the repo.
      
      ## Process
      
      ### 1. Scope the procedure
      
      Work out every manual step the human must take and every value that gets captured along the way. Read the repo first, don't ask cold:
      
      - For setup: `.env`, `.env.example`, `.env.*`, `README`, `docker-compose*`, framework config, and `.github/workflows/*` (every `secrets.*` / `vars.*` reference is a value the wizard must produce).
      - For a migration or transition: the current state, the target state, and the irreversible actions between them.
      
      Then show the user the ordered list of stages and the values each produces, and confirm: they may add, drop, or reorder.
      
      **Done when:** every stage is named in order, and for each captured value you know (a) where the human gets it, (b) where it's written (`.env`, a GitHub secret, both, or nowhere; some stages are pure actions), and (c) whether it's secret (hidden entry) or public.
      
      ### 2. Map each stage's journey
      
      For each stage, write the precise path a human follows: which URL to open, what to do there, where a value is shown, which variable it fills: e.g. "Dashboard → Developers → API keys → Reveal test key → copy". Where you don't actually know the current UI or the exact command, say so and ask the user or check the docs: never invent steps that may not exist.
      
      **Done when:** every stage traces to concrete instructions a stranger could follow.
      
      ### 3. Author the wizard
      
      Copy `template.sh` to the target path. Replace the example stage with one `stage` per step, in dependency order. Use the library helpers: `stage`, `say`/`step`, `open_url`, `ask`/`ask_secret`, `write_env`, `set_secret`/`set_var`, `pause`/`confirm`. Set `TOTAL_STAGES` to the number of stages you wrote.
      
      Hold the bar the template sets: open the URL before asking for its value, use `ask_secret` for anything secret, `write_env` every persisted value, `set_secret` only the values CI actually needs, and `confirm` before any irreversible action. Each `stage` clears the screen so only the current step is visible: keep a stage to one focused task so nothing the human needs scrolls away. Don't touch the library above the marker.
      
      ### 4. Verify and hand off
      
      - `bash -n <script>`; run `shellcheck` if available.
      - `chmod +x <script>`.
      - Don't run it end-to-end yourself: it opens browsers and blocks on human input. Trace it statically instead: every value from step 1 is captured and lands where step 1 said, and every `set_secret` name exactly matches a `secrets.*` reference in CI.
      - Tell the user how to run it. If it's a repeatable setup path, commit it and link it from the README so the next person runs the script instead of asking an AI.
      
  referencedBy: {}
````

````
- path: .agents/skills/teach/SKILL.md
  content: |
      ---
      name: teach
      description: Teach the user a new skill or concept, within this workspace.
      disable-model-invocation: true
      argument-hint: "What would you like to learn about?"
      ---
      
      The user has asked you to teach them something. This is a stateful request - they intend to learn the topic over multiple sessions.
      
      ## Teaching Workspace
      
      Treat the current directory as a teaching workspace. Workspace paths (`./lessons/` and the rest) resolve from the directory `/teach` was run in; only the `*-FORMAT.md` links resolve from this skill's folder. The state of their learning is captured in this directory in several files:
      
      - `MISSION.md`: A document capturing the _reason_ the user is interested in the topic. This should be used to ground all teaching. Use the format in [MISSION-FORMAT.md](./MISSION-FORMAT.md).
      - `./reference/*.html`: A directory of reference materials. These are the compressed learnings from the lessons - cheat sheets, reference algorithms, syntax, yoga poses, glossaries. They are the raw units of learning. They should be beautiful documents which print out well, and are designed for quick reference.
      - `RESOURCES.md`: A list of resources which can be explored to ground your teaching in contextual knowledge, or to acquire knowledge and wisdom. Use the format in [RESOURCES-FORMAT.md](./RESOURCES-FORMAT.md).
      - `./learning-records/*.md`: A directory of learning records, which capture what the user has learned. These are loosely equivalent to architectural decision records in software development - they capture non-obvious lessons and key insights that may need to be revised later, or drive future sessions. These should be used to calculate the zone of proximal development. They are titled `0001-<dash-case-name>.md`, where the number increments each time. Use the format in [LEARNING-RECORD-FORMAT.md](./LEARNING-RECORD-FORMAT.md).
      - `./lessons/*.html`: A directory of lessons. A **lesson** is a single, self-contained HTML output that teaches one tightly-scoped thing tied to the mission. This is the primary unit of teaching in this workspace.
      - `./assets/*`: Reusable **components** shared across lessons. See [Assets](#assets).
      - `NOTES.md`: A scratchpad for you to jot down user preferences, or working notes.
      
      ## Philosophy
      
      To learn at a deep level, the user needs three things:
      
      - **Knowledge**, captured from high-quality, high-trust resources
      - **Skills**, acquired through highly-relevant interactive lessons devised by you, based on the knowledge
      - **Wisdom**, which comes from interacting with other learners and practitioners
      
      Before the `RESOURCES.md` is well-populated, your focus should be to find high-quality resources which will help the user acquire knowledge. Never trust your parametric knowledge.
      
      Some topics may require more skills than knowledge. Learning more about theoretical physics might be more knowledge-based. For yoga, more skills-based.
      
      ### Fluency vs Storage Strength
      
      You should be careful to split between two types of learning:
      
      - **Fluency strength**: in-the-moment retrieval of knowledge
      - **Storage strength**: long-term retention of knowledge
      
      Fluency can give the user an illusory sense of mastery, but storage strength is the real goal. Try to design lessons which build long-term retention by desirable difficulty:
      
      - Using retrieval practice (recall from memory)
      - Spacing (distributing practice over time)
      - Interleaving (mixing up different but related topics in practice - for skills practice only)
      
      ## Lessons
      
      A lesson is the main thing you produce: the unit in which knowledge and skills reach the user. Each lesson is one self-contained HTML file, saved to `./lessons/` and titled `0001-<dash-case-name>.html` where the number increments each time.
      
      A lesson should be **beautiful**, with clean, readable typography and layout, since the user will return to these later to review. Think Tufte.
      
      The lesson should be short, and completable very quickly. Learners' working memory is very small, and we need to stay within it. But each lesson should give the user a single tangible win that they can build on. It should be directly tied to the mission, and should be in the user's zone of proximal development.
      
      If possible, open the lesson file for the user by running a CLI command.
      
      Each lesson should link via HTML anchors to other lessons and reference documents.
      
      Each lesson should recommend a primary source for the user to read or watch. This should be the most high-quality, high-trust resource you found on the topic.
      
      Each lesson should contain a reminder to ask followup questions to the agent. The agent is their teacher, and can assist with anything that's unclear.
      
      ## Assets
      
      Lessons are built from reusable **components**, stored in `./assets/`: stylesheets, quiz widgets, simulators, diagram helpers, and anything else a second lesson could reuse.
      
      Reuse is the default, not the exception. Before authoring a lesson, read `./assets/` and build from the components already there. When a lesson needs something new and reusable, write it as a component in `./assets/` and link to it; never inline code a future lesson would duplicate.
      
      A shared stylesheet is the first component every workspace earns: every lesson links it, so the lessons look like one consistent course rather than a pile of one-offs. As the workspace grows, so should the component library.
      
      ## The Mission
      
      Every lesson should be tied into the mission - the reason that the user is interested in learning about the topic.
      
      If the user is unclear about the mission, or the `MISSION.md` is not populated, your first job should be to question the user on why they want to learn this.
      
      Failing to understand the mission will mean knowledge acquisition is not grounded in real-world goals. Lessons will feel too abstract. You will have no way of judging what the user should do next.
      
      Missions may change as the user develops more skills and knowledge. This is normal - make sure to update the `MISSION.md` and add a learning record to capture the change. Confirm with the user before changing the mission.
      
      ## Zone Of Proximal Development
      
      Each lesson, the user should always feel as if they are being challenged 'just enough'.
      
      The user may specify an exact thing they want to learn. If they don't, figure out their zone of proximal development by:
      
      - Reading their `learning-records`
      - Figuring out the right thing to teach them based on their mission
      - Teach the most relevant thing that fits in their zone of proximal development
      
      ## Knowledge
      
      Lessons should be designed around a skill the user is going to learn. The knowledge in the lesson should be only what's required to acquire that skill. You teach the knowledge first, then get the user to practice the skills via an interactive feedback loop.
      
      Knowledge should first be gathered from trusted resources. Use `RESOURCES.md` to keep track of them. Lessons should be littered with citations - links to external resources to back up any claim made. This increases the trustworthiness of the lesson.
      
      For acquiring knowledge, difficulty is the enemy. It eats working memory you need for understanding.
      
      ## Skills
      
      If knowledge is all about acquisition, skills are about durability and flexibility. Make the knowledge stick.
      
      For skill acquisition, difficulty is the tool. Effortful retrieval is what builds storage strength. Skills should be taught through interactive lessons. There are several tools at your disposal:
      
      - Interactive lessons, using quizzes and light in-browser tasks
      - Lessons which guide the user through a list of real-world steps to take (for instance, yoga poses)
      
      Each of these should be based on a **feedback loop**, where the user receives feedback on their performance. This feedback loop should be as tight as possible, giving feedback immediately - and ideally automatically.
      
      For quizzes, each answer should be exactly the same number of words (and characters, if possible). Vary which position holds the correct answer across questions. Don't give the user any clues about the answer through formatting or order.
      
      ## Acquiring Wisdom
      
      Wisdom comes from true real-world interaction - testing your skills outside the learning environment.
      
      When the user asks a question that appears to require wisdom, your default posture should be to attempt to answer - but to ultimately delegate to a **community**.
      
      A community is a place (online or offline) where the user can test their skills in the real world. This might be a forum, a subreddit, a real-world class (budget permitting) or a local interest group.
      
      You should attempt to find high-reputation communities the user can join. If the user expresses a preference that they don't want to join a community, respect it.
      
      ## Reference Documents
      
      While creating lessons, you should also create reference documents. Lessons can reference these documents - they are useful for tracking raw units of knowledge useful across lessons.
      
      Lessons will rarely be revisited later - reference documents will be. They should be the compressed essence of the lesson, in a format designed for quick reference.
      
      Some learning topics lend themselves to reference:
      
      - Syntax and code snippets for programming
      - Algorithms and flowcharts for processes
      - Yoga poses and sequences for yoga
      - Exercises and routines for fitness
      - Glossaries for any topic with its own nomenclature
      
      Glossaries, in particular, are an essential reference. Once one is created, it should be adhered to in every lesson.
      
      ## `NOTES.md`
      
      The user will sometimes express preferences of how they want to be taught, or things you should keep in mind. This is the place to record those preferences, so you can refer back to them when designing lessons or working with the user.
      
  referencedBy: {}
- path: .agents/skills/scaffold-exercises/SKILL.md
  content: |
      ---
      name: scaffold-exercises
      description: Create exercise directory structures with sections, problems, solutions, and explainers that pass linting. Use when user wants to scaffold exercises, create exercise stubs, or set up a new course section.
      ---
      
      # Scaffold Exercises
      
      Create exercise directory structures that pass `pnpm ai-hero-cli internal lint`, then commit with `git commit`.
      
      ## Directory naming
      
      - **Sections**: `XX-section-name/` inside `exercises/` (e.g., `01-retrieval-skill-building`)
      - **Exercises**: `XX.YY-exercise-name/` inside a section (e.g., `01.03-retrieval-with-bm25`)
      - Section number = `XX`, exercise number = `XX.YY`
      - Names are dash-case (lowercase, hyphens)
      
      ## Exercise variants
      
      Each exercise needs at least one of these subfolders:
      
      - `problem/` - student workspace with TODOs
      - `solution/` - reference implementation
      - `explainer/` - conceptual material, no TODOs
      
      When stubbing, default to `explainer/` unless the plan specifies otherwise.
      
      ## Required files
      
      Each subfolder (`problem/`, `solution/`, `explainer/`) needs a `readme.md` that:
      
      - Is **not empty** (must have real content, even a single title line works)
      - Has no broken links
      
      When stubbing, create a minimal readme with a title and a description:
      
      ```md
      # Exercise Title
      
      Description here
      ```
      
      If the subfolder has code, it also needs a `main.ts` (>1 line). But for stubs, a readme-only exercise is fine.
      
      ## Workflow
      
      1. **Parse the plan** - extract section names, exercise names, and variant types
      2. **Create directories** - `mkdir -p` for each path
      3. **Create stub readmes** - one `readme.md` per variant folder with a title
      4. **Run lint** - `pnpm ai-hero-cli internal lint` to validate
      5. **Fix any errors** - iterate until lint passes
      
      ## Lint rules summary
      
      The linter (`pnpm ai-hero-cli internal lint`) checks:
      
      - Each exercise has subfolders (`problem/`, `solution/`, `explainer/`)
      - At least one of `problem/`, `explainer/`, or `explainer.1/` exists
      - `readme.md` exists and is non-empty in the primary subfolder
      - No `.gitkeep` files
      - No `speaker-notes.md` files
      - No broken links in readmes
      - No `pnpm run exercise` commands in readmes
      - `main.ts` required per subfolder unless it's readme-only
      
      ## Moving/renaming exercises
      
      When renumbering or moving exercises:
      
      1. Use `git mv` (not `mv`) to rename directories - preserves git history
      2. Update the numeric prefix to maintain order
      3. Re-run lint after moves
      
      Example:
      
      ```bash
      git mv exercises/01-retrieval/01.03-embeddings exercises/01-retrieval/01.04-embeddings
      ```
      
      ## Example: stubbing from a plan
      
      Given a plan like:
      
      ```
      Section 05: Memory Skill Building
      - 05.01 Introduction to Memory
      - 05.02 Short-term Memory (explainer + problem + solution)
      - 05.03 Long-term Memory
      ```
      
      Create:
      
      ```bash
      mkdir -p exercises/05-memory-skill-building/05.01-introduction-to-memory/explainer
      mkdir -p exercises/05-memory-skill-building/05.02-short-term-memory/{explainer,problem,solution}
      mkdir -p exercises/05-memory-skill-building/05.03-long-term-memory/explainer
      ```
      
      Then create readme stubs:
      
      ```
      exercises/05-memory-skill-building/05.01-introduction-to-memory/explainer/readme.md -> "# Introduction to Memory"
      exercises/05-memory-skill-building/05.02-short-term-memory/explainer/readme.md -> "# Short-term Memory"
      exercises/05-memory-skill-building/05.02-short-term-memory/problem/readme.md -> "# Short-term Memory"
      exercises/05-memory-skill-building/05.02-short-term-memory/solution/readme.md -> "# Short-term Memory"
      exercises/05-memory-skill-building/05.03-long-term-memory/explainer/readme.md -> "# Long-term Memory"
      ```
````

Now I have the agent-skills pattern fully mapped. Here's what the real structure teaches us, then round 2 of the grilling.

---

**What I found in `.agents/skills/` (the agent-skills model):**

| Element | What it is in agent-skills | What it maps to in our task runner |
|---|---|---|
| One folder per skill, e.g. `.agents/skills/implement/` | A **skill package** | A **task package** (a bun/npm package that can contain multiple tasks) |
| `SKILL.md` at folder root | The **manifest + instructions** — name, description, and the procedural body an agent reads at runtime | The task's `README.md` + manifest (`settings.json`) — documentation for humans *and* the runner's metadata |
| `agents/openai.yaml` inside each skill | Per-agent **interface config** — `display_name`, `short_description`, invocation policy. One YAML per agent runtime. | Per-runtime/task **interface descriptor** — how this task exposes itself to a given runner/daemon. One descriptor per "agent" (runtime) that wants to run the task |
| `scripts/`, `template.sh` | Executable/bulk assets the skill uses | Task **assets** — the actual `default.ts` entrypoint, helper scripts, media files |
| `skills-lock.json` | **Lockfile** mapping skill name → source + installed path. This is the `$PATH`-like index the system reads to know what's available | Our **task registry index** — maps `namespace:task-id` → package location, so the daemon can resolve `tq add ls ...` by walking the index |
| `npx skills add <package>` / `skills-lock.json` + `experimental_sync` | Add a package; lock it; optionally sync installed files into agent dirs | Install a task package; record it in a lockfile; the daemon reads the lockfile/index to discover tasks |
| `npx skills find` | **Search/discover** skills from a registry | Our **task discovery** — list available tasks across default + user task folders |
| The skill's *name* is declared **inside** `SKILL.md` (`name: implement`), not derived from the folder name | Identity is **declared**, folder name is just install location | A task's ID/namespace should be **declared in its manifest**, not purely inferred from the folder name (folder can be `tasks/minfo/` but the task declares `namespace: default, id: minfo` — or whatever) |

**The multi-task-in-one-package lesson:** a single installed package is one folder, and that folder is one skill. To have a package expose *multiple* tasks, the agent-skills model would install *multiple folders* (one per task) — either as separate sub-packages or as a monorepo where each task is its own folder under a shared package root. The `scaffold-exercises` skill is a great analog: it has a `scaffold-exercises/` package root and inside it creates *many* exercise folders (`01-retrieval-skill-building/01.03-retrieval-with-bm25/problem/`, etc.), each a self-contained unit with its own `readme.md`. That's exactly the shape: **package root = shared config/lib; each task = a subfolder with its own `default.ts` + `README.md` + `settings.json`**.

So for Q2/Q4 together: a task package is a bun package whose `tasks/` directory holds one subfolder per task; shared code lives in the package root (or a `lib/` dir) and each task imports it via relative path `../../lib/foo`. The daemon's task-path lookup walks installed package locations and their `tasks/` subfolders, building the `$PATH`-like index from each task's declared manifest.

---

❓ **Q10** - **Namespace syntax and the manifest's declared identity**:

Agent skills declare `name:` inside `SKILL.md`, and the install folder name can differ from the declared name. I recommend we follow that: a task's **identity** (namespace + id) is *declared in its manifest*, and the folder name is just where it's installed.

- **Q10a** — Namespace syntax: `namespace:id` with `:` separator (e.g. `default:minfo`, `acme:encode`), or something else? And is the `default` namespace special (the built-in/user task folders you mentioned), or just the first namespace?
- **Q10b** — Does the manifest file live at the *task folder* root (e.g. `tasks/minfo/settings.json` declares `{ namespace: "default", id: "minfo", ... }`), or at the *package* root (one manifest for the whole package, with a `tasks[]` listing each task folder)?

➡️ **Q10a**: `namespace:id` with `:` — it's cheap now, pays off immediately once two packages both want `encode`. `default` is the namespace for the built-in task folders + user's `~/tasks` or `~/.taskrunner/tasks`; other namespaces come from installed packages that opt into a namespace. ➡️ **Q10b**: Manifest per *task folder* (`tasks/<id>/settings.json`), because each task has its own concurrency, its own input schema, its own README — per-package manifest would force a one-settings-fits-all shape that doesn't match "each registered task correspond to a task queue". A *package* can have a lightweight `package.json`-style manifest for the package itself (name, version, dependencies, and a `tasks: ["minfo", "ffprobe"]` index), but the *task identity + settings* live in each task folder's manifest.

---

❓ **Q11** - **The task interface descriptor (the `agents/openai.yaml` analog)**:

In agent-skills, `agents/openai.yaml` is per-agent-runtime config: `display_name`, `short_description`, `policy.allow_implicit_invocation`. This is the key design idea: a skill describes *how it presents to a given agent runtime*, and a different runtime could have a different descriptor.

- **Q11a** — Do we want a similar per-"runtime" descriptor for tasks, or is one task manifest enough? Concretely: is there ever a case where the *same task* needs a different description/metadata depending on who's running it (e.g. the web dashboard shows one label, the CLI shows another, an external API consumer sees a third)?
- **Q11b** — If yes, do we model it as `agents/<runtime>.json` inside each task folder (mirroring agent-skills), or fold it into the one manifest with a `runtimes:` block?

➡️ **Q11a**: Yes, keep the analog — it's cheap and the agent-skills pattern is right here. The web dashboard and the CLI and any future API consumer are different "runtimes" that may want slightly different metadata (e.g. dashboard-friendly description, CLI `argument-hint`, icon, etc.). ➡️ **Q11b**: Mirror agent-skills: `agents/<runtime>.json` per runtime. Start with `agents/daemon.json` (the daemon's view: concurrency, input schema ref, log path, status) and `agents/cli.json` (CLI view: argument hint, short description, example). One manifest (`settings.json`) holds the *task's own* settings (concurrency, env, schedule ref); the runtime descriptors are separate and optional. If a runtime descriptor is missing, the daemon falls back to `settings.json` fields.

---

❓ **Q12** - **Input schema location and validation**:

You said the task defines its input convention, and I recommended the manifest/schema declares the shape so the registry can validate at `tq add` time. Two sub-questions:

- **Q12a** — Where does the input schema live? In `settings.json` (a JSON Schema embedded as a JSON value), in a separate `schema.ts` / `schema.json` next to `default.ts`, or inferred from the task function's TypeScript signature (the daemon reads `default(filepath: string)` and derives `{ filepath: string }`)?
- **Q12b** — At `tq add ls fixture/1`, the context is a string. Does the daemon: (i) parse it as JSON and validate against the schema, (ii) treat a non-JSON string as a *positional* value matched to the schema's first positional field, or (iii) pass the raw string and let the task parse it?

➡️ **Q12a**: Schema in the task folder as `schema.json` (JSON Schema draft-07). `settings.json` references it (`"inputSchema": "schema.json"`) or inlines a simple shape. We don't infer from TS signatures — that breaks when tasks are JS, when the signature is generic, or when the input is richer than the function param. `schema.json` is explicit and validate-able by the daemon without compiling the task. ➡️ **Q12b**: (ii) + (i): if the context string is valid JSON, parse + validate against the schema. If it's a plain string, treat it as the value for the schema's first *positional* (non-object) field — so `tq add ls fixture/1` with schema `{ type: "object", properties: { filepath: { type: "string" } }, required: ["filepath"] }` would... actually no. Let me correct: for a string schema (`{ type: "string" }`), a plain argument is the value directly. For an object schema, a plain string argument is an error unless the schema declares a single positional string field. Best to be explicit: `tq add ls '{"filepath":"fixture/1"}'` for object inputs, and tasks that want a bare string declare `{ type: "string" }`. So `ls` and `minfo` would declare `{ type: "object", properties: { filepath: { type: "string" } }, required: ["filepath"] }` and users pass JSON. *But* that's verbose for the common case — so the CLI could have a convenience: `tq add ls fixture/1` auto-wraps to `{"filepath":"fixture/1"}` when the schema has exactly one required string property. I recommend that convenience, but the *authoritative* input is always JSON validated against the schema.

---

❓ **Q13** - **Task execution contract: what the `default` function signs and how the runner talks to it**:

You said *"each task has a skill, the system won't do scheduling, the task's execution can implement scheduling"*. So the task is in charge of its own execution logic; the runner just launches it.

- **Q13a** — What does the runner pass to the task, and what does the task return? Options:
  - (i) `default(input: TaskInput) → Promise<number>` (exit code; runner maps 0→SUCCESS, non-zero→FAILED; stdout/stderr captured by the runner)
  - (ii) `default(ctx: ExecutionContext) → Promise<void>` where `ctx` gives the task `ctx.stdin`/`ctx.stdout`/`ctx.stderr` streams, `ctx.abortSignal`, `ctx.log()`, and the task is fully in charge of its own output and exit status
  - (iii) `default(input, config) → Promise<Result>` where `Result = { exitCode, stdout, stderr, duration }` — the task returns its own result
- **Q13b** — Since you said the task implements its own scheduling: does that mean a task's `default` can be *long-running* (a daemon-like loop that the runner starts once and the task itself manages its own sub-work), or is each `default` call a single atomic job the runner treats as one task instance?

➡️ **Q13a**: (ii) — `default(ctx: ExecutionContext) → Promise<void>`. The runner gives the task: the parsed input, an `AbortSignal` (for `abort`), writable streams / log callbacks for stdout/stderr (so the runner captures them for the dashboard), and a `ctx.done(exitCode)` callback or thrown error to signal completion. This keeps the runner in charge of *observability* (capturing logs, timing, state transitions) while the task is in charge of *execution*. The current `ls.ts`/`minfo.ts` are `default(filepath)` returning a number — we adapt them to the new contract (thin wrapper). ➡️ **Q13b**: Each `default` call = one atomic task instance. "The task implements its own scheduling" means the task's *logic* can internally schedule sub-work (e.g. the task could, as part of its body, decide to process a batch of files, or poll something) — but from the runner's view it's one instance with one start and one finish. If you want a truly long-running task (a persistent worker), that's a different concept — let's call it a *service task* and treat it as future scope. For now, one instance = one `default` invocation = one PENDING→RUNNING→SUCCESS/FAILED lifecycle.

---

❓ **Q14** - **SQLite persistence: what goes in the DB vs. what stays as files**:

You said persist to SQLite via Bun's API. Bun's SQLite is `bun:sqlite`. Two sub-questions:

- **Q14a** — Does the SQLite DB hold *everything* (task instances, their state, their logs, the task registry index, queue state), or does it hold *metadata* while logs live as files on disk (the existing `.gitignore` already has `logs/` and `*.log`)?
- **Q14b** — Where does the DB file live? A single `taskrunner.db` in the daemon's working dir, a configurable path (`--db`), or in a standard location (`~/.taskrunner/taskrunner.db`)?

➡️ **Q14a**: SQLite holds metadata + state: task definitions registry (id, namespace, package ref, folder path, manifest hash), task instances (id, task-id, input, state, exitCode, startedAt, finishedAt, duration, error), queue state (queue-id, state, concurrency, current running count). Logs (stdout/stderr) live as files in `logs/<instance-id>.log` (or `.stdout`/`.stderr`), and the DB references the log path. Reasoning: logs can be large; SQLite is for structured lookups and dashboard queries, not log storage. The dashboard reads log paths from the DB and streams the file. ➡️ **Q14b**: Configurable, default `./taskrunner.db` in the daemon's working directory (so `tq daemon` from a project dir keeps its DB alongside the project — nice for per-project task runners). A `--db` flag or `TASKRUNNER_DB` env overrides it.

---

❓ **Q15** - **Queue state semantics and the start/stop/abort operations**:

Your spec: queue states IDLE/PROCESSING/STOPPED; `stop` = finish current RUNNING, don't start next PENDING; `abort` = kill current RUNNING, reset to PENDING, don't start next PENDING.

- **Q15a** — When a queue is STOPPED and then `tq start` again: does it resume from where it left off (the PENDING tasks that were queued remain PENDING and get picked up), or does "start" mean something else?
- **Q15b** — `abort` resets the aborted instance to PENDING. Does that mean the same instance ID is reused and re-run with the same input, or is a *new* instance created? (I.e., is abort "put it back in the queue to retry" or "cancel and the user re-adds"?)
- **Q15c** — Concurrency: when a RUNNING instance finishes and the queue is below its concurrency limit, does the runner *immediately* pick the next PENDING, or does it respect some ordering (FIFO, priority, insertion order)?

➡️ **Q15a**: Resume. STOPPED is a pause; PENDING tasks stay PENDING; `start` resumes draining them in order. IDLE = queue exists but has no RUNNING tasks and isn't currently processing (e.g. empty queue, or freshly created). PROCESSING = actively draining. So the state machine is: IDLE ⇄ PROCESSING (when tasks are running), and PROCESSING→STOPPED (on `stop`), STOPPED→PROCESSING (on `start`). IDLE→PROCESSING happens when a task is added to an idle queue and auto-start kicks in. ➡️ **Q15b**: Abort = same instance, state rolled back to PENDING, input preserved, partial logs preserved (marked). The instance gets re-picked and re-run with the same input on next drain. This is "retry the same job". If you want cancel-and-discard, that's a separate `tq cancel` later. ➡️ **Q15c**: FIFO / insertion order. The PENDING queue is a FIFO. When a slot opens and the queue is PROCESSING, pop the next PENDING and launch it, up to concurrency. No priority for now; insertion order is the default and matches `tq add` order.

---

❓ **Q16** - **The `tq clear` semantics and task instance lifecycle after SUCCESS/FAILED**:

Your spec: `tq clear ls` clears SUCCESS tasks; `tq clear --all ls` clears all tasks.

- **Q16a** — What does "clear" mean operationally? Delete the instance rows from SQLite (and their log files)? Archive them? Just filter them out of the dashboard view? And what about FAILED tasks — does `clear --all` remove FAILED too, or only SUCCESS by default?
- **Q16b** — After a task is "cleared", can it still be referenced (e.g. `tq logs <id>`)? Or is clear destructive?

➡️ **Q16a**: Clear = delete from SQLite + delete log files (destructive, with a confirmation for `--all`). `tq clear ls` = delete SUCCESS instances only for queue `ls`. `tq clear --all ls` = delete ALL instances (SUCCESS + FAILED + PENDING + RUNNING? — RUNNING can't be deleted safely, so `--all` clears SUCCESS+FAILED+PENDING and refuses/aborts RUNNING). Failed tasks are *not* cleared by plain `clear`; you need `--all` or a `tq clear --failed` if we add it. ➡️ **Q16b**: Clear is destructive; after clear, the instance ID is gone and `tq logs <id>` returns "not found". If you want non-destructive archive, that's `tq archive` later. For now, clear = delete.

---

❓ **Q17** - **Dashboard real-time channel and the web UI shape**:

You said web UI, daemon-served, real-time updates. Two sub-questions:

- **Q17a** — Real-time via SSE (Server-Sent Events, one-way daemon→client) or WebSocket (bidirectional)? SSE is simpler and matches "dashboard shows state + log tail". WebSocket is more flexible if you want the dashboard to *issue commands* (start/stop/abort) through the socket instead of the CLI.
- **Q17b** — Does the dashboard serve *only* live data (the page connects, gets current state, then subscribes to updates), or does it also expose REST endpoints for the CLI and API consumers (`GET /queues`, `GET /queues/:id/tasks`, `POST /queues/:id/start`, etc.)?

➡️ **Q17a**: SSE for log tailing and state change events (simple, Bun.serve supports it naturally, one-directional is enough for "show me logs + state"). Commands still go through CLI or a REST API — not through the SSE channel. ➡️ **Q17b**: Yes, expose a small REST API on the same HTTP server: `GET /` (dashboard HTML), `GET /api/queues`, `GET /api/queues/:id`, `GET /api/queues/:id/tasks`, `GET /api/tasks/:id/logs` (or `/api/tasks/:id/logs/stream` for SSE), `POST /api/queues/:id/start`, `POST /api/queues/:id/stop`, `POST /api/queues/:id/abort`, `POST /api/tasks` (add task — the API equivalent of `tq add`). The CLI `tq` commands become thin clients over this API *or* direct DB/process calls — we decide in the next round.

---

❓ **Q18** - **`tq` CLI implementation: CLI as REST client vs. CLI as in-process calls**:

- **Q18a** — Does `tq` (the CLI) talk to a *running daemon* over HTTP (so `tq add` is really `POST /api/tasks`), or does `tq` talk directly to the SQLite DB / invoke the daemon's logic in-process (so `tq` and `tq daemon` are the same binary, and `tq add` writes to the DB that the daemon reads)?
- **Q18b** — If HTTP: does `tq` need the daemon to be running to work (fail if not reachable), or does `tq add` write to the DB directly and the daemon picks it up (so `tq add` works without the daemon)?

➡️ **Q18a**: Same binary, two modes. `tq daemon` starts the HTTP server + task runner loop. `tq <subcommand>` (add, stop, start, abort, clear, status, logs) works *both* ways: if the daemon is running, it calls the REST API; if the daemon is not running, it operates directly on the SQLite DB (write for `add`/`clear`, read for `status`/`logs`). This is the "CLI as a thin client that degrades to direct DB access" model — robust and nice to use. ➡️ **Q18b**: `tq add` writes to the DB directly (no daemon needed). The daemon, when it starts or when a queue is PROCESSING, reads PENDING tasks from the DB and drains them. So you can add tasks before starting the daemon; they sit in the DB as PENDING and get picked up on `tq daemon` or `tq start`. This matches "registered tasks should autostart" — on `tq daemon`, it starts processing what's already in the DB.

---

❓ **Q19** - **Where `default` tasks run: same process or child process, and the concurrency/isolation model**:

I recommended a single daemon process. But you said in Q1: *"the daemon forks task instances to run the command line in the background"*. This raises an isolation question:

- **Q19a** — When the runner launches a task instance, does it run `default(ctx)` *in the same Bun process* (as a concurrent coroutine/task), or does it *spawn a child process* (a new `bun run <task>` invocation) so that a crashing task can't take down the daemon?
- **Q19b** — If same-process: how do we guarantee one task's crash (unhandled error, infinite loop, memory leak) doesn't corrupt the daemon? Bun's `task` primitives / `async` + `AbortSignal` + try/catch can isolate *logic errors*, but a memory leak or infinite sync loop is harder. Is same-process acceptable with the understanding that tasks are trusted code, or do we want process isolation from the start?

➡️ **Q19a**: Same-process, using Bun's concurrency primitives. The runner launches `default(ctx)` as an async task with an `AbortSignal`; the runner catches errors, captures stdout/stderr via the `ctx` log callbacks/streams, and transitions state. This keeps things simple and leverages Bun's strengths. ➡️ **Q19b**: Same-process with the understanding that *tasks are trusted code* (they're installed by the user, like shell scripts or agent skills). Process isolation is a future hardening step if needed. The `AbortSignal` gives us clean aborts; unhandled errors are caught by the runner wrapper; infinite loops would hang the instance and require abort. If we later want isolation, we can spawn child processes per instance without changing the task contract. For now: same-process, trusted tasks, runner wraps every invocation in error handling + abortability.

---

❓ **Q20** - **Task package install/discovery mechanics: how a package gets onto the task path**:

The agent-skills model: `npx skills add <package>` → writes `skills-lock.json` → files land in `.agents/skills/<name>/`. The system then discovers from the lockfile + installed folders.

- **Q20a** — Do we want an analogous `tq add-plugin <package>` (or `tq install <package>`) that fetches a bun package, extracts its `tasks/` directory into a known location, and updates a lockfile/index? Or do we keep it filesystem-based: the user drops a package folder into a task path, and the daemon discovers it on restart/reload?
- **Q20b** — What are the task search paths (the `$PATH` analogs)? Candidates: (i) a built-in `tasks/` in the project (like the existing `tasks/minfo.ts`, `tasks/ls.ts` — but now as folders), (ii) `~/taskrunner/tasks` or `~/.taskrunner/tasks` for user-global tasks, (iii) installed package locations recorded in a lockfile. Which of these are search paths, and in what order?

➡️ **Q20a**: Both, but start simple. A `tq install <package>` command that does the bun-package install + extraction + index update is the polished path. But the *minimum* is: the daemon discovers task folders by walking its search paths on start (and on `tq reload` or SIGHUP). So a user can manually drop a task package folder into a search path and reload. The `tq install` command is a convenience that automates the drop + lockfile update. Agent-skills' `skills-lock.json` is a good model for the lockfile — we have `taskrunner-lock.json` (or `.taskrunner.lock`) mapping `namespace:id` → source + installed path + manifest hash. ➡️ **Q20b**: Three search paths, in order: (1) **project tasks** — `<project>/tasks/` (the existing location; a package can have its own `tasks/` with multiple task subfolders); (2) **user tasks** — `~/.taskrunner/tasks/` (user-global, like `~/bin`); (3) **installed packages** — locations recorded in the lockfile, typically `node_modules/<pkg>/tasks/` or a extracted location. The daemon walks these in order and builds the namespace:id index; the first task folder that declares a given `namespace:id` wins (so user tasks can shadow project tasks, etc.). The `default` namespace is special only in that the project + user paths are the "default" search locations; installed packages can declare any namespace.

Glossary\n\n- **Task definition** — a registered task: its identity (`namespace:id`), input schema, settings, entrypoint, README, assets.\n- **Task instance** — one execution of a task definition: a PENDING → RUNNING → SUCCESS/FAILED lifecycle with captured stdout/stderr, timing, and exit code.\n- **Task queue** — one per task definition. Architecture — settled decisions\n\n### 3.1 Process model\n\n- Single daemon process (`tq daemon`).\n- Task instances run as concurrent coroutines **in the same process**, launched by the runner with an `AbortSignal` and wrapped in error handling.\n- Tasks are **trusted code** (installed by the user).
